```cpp
133: Tensor maybe_multiply(const Tensor& t, const Scalar& s) {
134:   bool is_one = false;
135:   if (s.isFloatingPoint()) {
136:     is_one = s.toSymFloat() == 1;
137:   } else if (s.isIntegral(true)) {
138:     is_one = s.toSymInt() == 1;
139:   }
140: 
141:   if (is_one) {
142:     return t;
143:   } else {
144:     return t * s;
145:   }
146: }
```

```cpp
290: std::tuple<Tensor, Tensor> _euclidean_dist_backward(
291:     const Tensor& grad,
292:     const Tensor& x1,
293:     const Tensor& x2,
294:     const Tensor& res) {
295:   if (!grad.defined()) {
296:     return std::tuple<Tensor, Tensor>(Tensor(), Tensor());
297:   }
298:   // handle case at 0 where we return a subgradient containing 0
299:   Tensor ratio = grad / res;
300:   ratio.masked_fill_(res == 0, 0);
301:   return std::tuple<Tensor, Tensor>{
302:       x1 * ratio.sum(-1, true) - ratio.matmul(x2),
303:       x2 * ratio.sum(-2, false).unsqueeze(-1) - ratio.mT().matmul(x1)};
304: }
```

```cpp
337:   if (p == 0.0) {

343:   } else if (std::isinf(p)) {
344:     // Derivative of amax(abs(self), dim, keepdim) but respecting nans

347:     auto self_abs = self.abs();
348:     auto mask = self_abs.eq(norm).logical_or(self_abs.isnan());
349:     return self.sgn() * ((grad / mask.sum(dim, true)) * mask);

351:     self_scaled =

362:     scale_v.masked_fill_(norm == 0, 0);
363:     return self_scaled * scale_v;
```

```cpp
486:   }
487:

490:         (grads[0].dim() == 1 ? grads[0].unsqueeze(0) : grads[0])
491:             .matmul(weight.mT());

493:   if (grads[1].defined()) {
494:     grad_grad_output = grad_grad_output +

501:     grad_grad_output = grad_grad_output.squeeze(0);

503:
```

```cpp
733: Tensor unsqueeze_multiple(
734:     const Tensor& t,
735:     OptionalIntArrayRef opt_dim,
736:     size_t n_dims) {
737:   if (opt_dim.has_value()) {
738:     IntArrayRef dim = opt_dim.value();
739:     auto dim_size = dim.size();
740:     // Optimisation for two common cases
741:     if (dim_size == 0) {
742:       return t;
743:     } else if (dim_size == 1) {
744:       return t.unsqueeze(dim[0]);
745:     }
746:   }
747:   auto dims_to_unsqueeze = at::dim_list_to_bitset(opt_dim, n_dims);
748:   Tensor res = t;
749:   for (const auto i : c10::irange(n_dims)) {
750:     if (dims_to_unsqueeze[i]) {
751:       res = res.unsqueeze(static_cast<int64_t>(i));
752:     }
753:   }
754:   return res;
755: }
```

```cpp
930:   return solve(A, dB, dA_contrib);
```

```cpp
1236: Tensor clamp_backward(
1237:     const Tensor& grad,
1238:     const Tensor& self,
1239:     const std::optional<Scalar>& min,
1240:     const std::optional<Scalar>& max) {
1241:   // clamp: gradients not defined on min and max, so we return the subgradient 1
1242:   // for these cases.
1243:   if (max && min) {
1244:     auto zero = at::scalar_tensor(0., grad.options());
1245:     return where((self >= *min).logical_and_(self <= *max), grad, zero);
1246:   } else if (min) {
1247:     auto zero = at::scalar_tensor(0., grad.options());
1248:     return where(self >= *min, grad, zero);
1249:   } else if (max) {
1250:     auto zero = at::scalar_tensor(0., grad.options());
1251:     return where(self <= *max, grad, zero);
1252:   } else {
1253:     return grad;
1254:   }
1255: }
```

```cpp
1613: static Tensor sparse_mask_like_grad(
1614:     const Tensor& x,
1615:     const Tensor& gx,
1616:     bool accumulate_matches) {
1617:   if (x.is_coalesced() && gx.is_coalesced()) {
1618:     if (x._nnz() >= gx._nnz()) {
1619:       // search into x is faster
1620:       return gx._sparse_mask_projection(x, accumulate_matches);
1621:     } else {
1622:       // search into gx is faster
1623:       return gx.sparse_mask(x);
1624:     }
1625:   } else if (x.is_coalesced()) {
1626:     return gx.sparse_mask(x);
1627:   } else if (gx.is_coalesced()) {
1628:     return gx._sparse_mask_projection(x, accumulate_matches);
1629:   } else {
1630:     if (x._nnz() >= gx._nnz()) {
1631:       // gx.coalesce() is likely faster
1632:       return gx.coalesce()._sparse_mask_projection(x, accumulate_matches);
1633:     } else {
1634:       // x.coalesce() is likely faster
1635:       return gx.sparse_mask(x.coalesce());
1636:     }
1637:   }
1638: }
```

```cpp
1679: Tensor sparse_sparse_matmul_backward(
1680:     const Tensor& grad,
1681:     const Tensor& a,
1682:     const Tensor& b,
1683:     int64_t grad_order) {
1684:   /*
1685:   To implement the backward algorithm for sparse matrix-matrix matmul (SPMM) we
1686:   can start from the following definition for dense tensors:
1687: 
1688:   c = a @ b
1689:       then
1690:   a_grad = c_grad @ b^H
1691:   b_grad = a^H @ c_grad
1692: 
1693:   So for sparse matrices we can use the following definition:
1694: 
1695:   if grad_order == 0:
1696:       a_grad = sparse_matrix_mask(c_grad @ b^H, mask=a)
1697:   else:
1698:       b_grad = sparse_matrix_mask(a^H @ c_grad, mask=b)
1699:   */
1700:   TORCH_CHECK(
1701:       grad_order == 0 || grad_order == 1,
1702:       ": grad_order not in [0, 1] at sparse_sparse_matmul_backward function");
1703: 
1704:   // NOTE: _sparse_sparse_matmul returns a coalesced gradient,
1705:   //   // hence there is no need in accumulating matches.
1706:   if (grad_order == 0) {
1707:     auto a_grad = _sparse_sparse_matmul(grad, b.conj().t());
1708:     return sparse_mask_like_grad(a, a_grad, /*accumulate_matches=*/false);
1709:   }
1710:   auto b_grad = _sparse_sparse_matmul(a.conj().t(), grad);
1711:   return sparse_mask_like_grad(b, b_grad, /*accumulate_matches=*/false);
1712: }
```

```cpp
1937: Tensor var_backward(
1938:     Tensor grad,
1939:     const Tensor& self,
1940:     at::OptionalIntArrayRef dim_opt,
1941:     const std::optional<at::Scalar>& correction_opt,
1942:     bool keepdim) {
1943:   const auto correction = correction_opt.value_or(1).toSymFloat();
1944:   if (self.dim() == 0 || !dim_opt.has_value()) {
1945:     const auto dof = c10::SymFloat(self.sym_numel()) - correction;
1946:     if (dof <= 0) {
1947:       // when n == correction, 2 / (n - correction) is infinity
1948:       // when self == self.mean(), we return NaN because infinity * 0 = NaN
1949:       // otherwise, we return infinity because infinity * c = infinity, for all
1950:       // c > 0
1951:       return grad *
1952:           at::where(
1953:                  self == self.mean(),
1954:                  std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN(),
1955:                  std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity());
1956:     } else {
1957:       return (c10::SymFloat(2.0) / dof) * grad * (self - self.mean());
1958:     }
1959:   }
1960:   auto dim = dim_opt.value();
1961:   if (!keepdim && self.dim() > 1) {
1962:     grad = unsqueeze_multiple(grad, dim, self.dim());
1963:   }
1964:   const c10::SymFloat rnumel(_safe_size(self.sym_sizes(), dim));
1965:   return (c10::SymFloat(2.0) / (rnumel - correction)) * grad *
1966:       (self - self.mean(dim, /*keepdim=*/true));
1967: }
1968: 
1969: Tensor std_backward(
1970:     const Tensor& result,
1971:     const Tensor& grad,
1972:     const Tensor& self,
1973:     at::OptionalIntArrayRef dim,
1974:     const std::optional<c10::Scalar>& correction_opt,
1975:     bool keepdim) {
1976:   auto grad_var = (grad / (result * 2)).masked_fill_(result == 0, 0);
1977:   return var_backward(std::move(grad_var), self, dim, correction_opt, keepdim);
1978: }
```

```cpp
2027: Tensor cholesky_jvp(const Tensor& dA, const Tensor& L, bool upper) {
2028:   at::NoTF32Guard disable_tf32;
2029:   // Let A = LL^H
2030:   // dA = dLL^H + L(dL)^H
2031:   // L^{-1}dA(L^{-H}) = L^{-1}dL + (L^{-1}dL)^H
2032:   //               = sym(L^{-1}dL)
2033:   // where sym(X) = X + X^H
2034:   // A short computation gives that the inverse of sym is given by
2035:   // \pi(X) = X.tril() - 0.5*diag(X)
2036:   // so
2037:   // dL = L\pi(L^{-1}dA(L^{-H}))
2038: 
2039:   // Precondition: dA is symmetric/Hermitian
2040:   auto L_ = upper ? L.mH() : L;
2041:   auto dL = at::linalg_solve_triangular(L_, dA, /*upper=*/false, /*left=*/true);
2042:   dL = at::linalg_solve_triangular(L_.mH(), dL, /*upper=*/true, /*left=*/false);
2043:   dL = dL.tril() - dL.diagonal(0, -2, -1).mul(0.5).diag_embed();
2044:   dL = L_.matmul(dL);
2045:   return upper ? dL.mH() : std::move(dL);
2046: }
```

```cpp
2049:   at::NoTF32Guard disable_tf32;
2050:   // From cholesky_jvp we have that
2051:   // dL = L\pi(L^{-1}dA(L^-H))
2052:   //
2053:   // Let gL be the projection into the lower-triangular gradient wrt L. Taking
2054:   // adjoints we have gA = L^{-H}\pi^*((L^HgL).tril())L^{-1} where \pi^*(X) =
2055:   // 0.5 * (X + X^H - diag(X)) The only non-standard point of this derivation is
2056:   // noting that the adjoint to multiplying on the left by a lower triangular
2057:   // matrix L is multiplying by L^H and then projecting back to the lower
2058:   // triangular matrices (hence the .tril() projection) Note that the gradient
2059:   // is symmetric and not triangular.
2060:   auto L_ = upper ? L.mH() : L;
2061:   auto gL_ = upper ? gL.mH() : gL;
2062: 
2063:   // Nb. We don't need to compute gL_ = gL.tril() as
2064:   // tril(L^H gL) = tril(L^H (triu(gL, 1) + tril(gL)))
2065:   //              = tril(L^H tril(gL)) + tril(L^H triu(gL, 1))
2066:   //              = tril(L^H tril(gL))
2067:   // since tril(L^H triu(gL, 1)) = 0, as L^H triu(gL, 1) is upper triangular
2068:   auto gA = L_.mH().matmul(gL_).tril();
2069:   // Equivalent to 0.5 * (gA + gA^H - diag(gA))
2070:   gA = 0.5 * (gA + gA.tril(-1).mH());
2071:   gA = at::linalg_solve_triangular(L_.mH(), gA, /*upper=*/true, /*left=*/true);
2072:   gA = at::linalg_solve_triangular(L_, gA, /*upper=*/false, /*left=*/false);
2073:   return gA;
2074: }
```

```cpp
2117: // The formula for forward AD is adapted from
2118: //
2119: // Golub, Gene H., and Victor Pereyra. "The Differentiation of Pseudo-Inverses
2120: // and Nonlinear Least Squares Problems Whose Variables Separate." SIAM Journal
2121: // on Numerical Analysis 10(2). (1973). 413-432. doi: 10.1137/0710036
2122: //
2123: // We present a short derivation below:
2124: // Let Ap := pinv(A), then Ap is the unique matrix such that
2125: //
2126: // Ap A Ap = Ap [1]
2127: // A Ap A = A   [2]
2128: //
2129: // By differentiating [1] we get:
2130: //
2131: // dAp = dAp A Ap + Ap dA Ap + Ap A dAp [3]
2132: //
2133: // In the rhs of [3] the products involving dAp could be expressed as products
2134: // of Ap^i, A^j, dA^k with i, j, k in {1, H}, where X^H = X.mH(). To prove that,
2135: // note (A Ap)^H = A Ap and (Ap A)^H = Ap A, which could be shown by taking the
2136: // product between the SVD decompositions of A and Ap. Consider the
2137: // conjugate-transposed [2]: (A Ap A)^H = A^H (A Ap) = A^H. By differentiating
2138: // it we get: dA^H A Ap + A^H dA Ap + A^H A dAp = dA^H. By multiplying from the
2139: // left by Ap^H and using Ap^H A^H = (A Ap)^H = A Ap: Ap^H dA^H A Ap + A Ap dA
2140: // Ap + A Ap A dAp = Ap^H dA^H. By multiplying from the left by Ap and by
2141: // applying [1] and [2] repeatedly until impossible we get: Ap Ap^H dA^H A Ap +
2142: // Ap dA Ap + Ap A dAp = Ap Ap^H dA^H. By rearranging the terms:
2143: //
2144: // Ap A dAp = -Ap dA Ap + Ap Ap^H dA^H (I - A Ap) [4],
2145: // which is one of the summands in [3].
2146: //
2147: // Similar, by differentiating the transpose-conjugated [2] written differently,
2148: // i.e. (A Ap A)^H = Ap A A^H = A^H we will get an expression for dAp A Ap,
2149: // which is
2150: //
2151: // dAp A Ap = -Ap dA Ap + (I - Ap A) dA^H Ap^H Ap [5].
2152: //
2153: // By plugging in [4] and [5] into [3] we get the forward AD formula for pinv:
2154: //
2155: // dAp = -Ap dA Ap + (I - Ap A) dA^H Ap^H Ap + Ap Ap^H dA^H (I - A Ap).
2156: Tensor pinv_jvp(const Tensor& A, const Tensor& pinvA, const Tensor& dA) {
2157:   at::NoTF32Guard disable_tf32;
2158:   auto m = A.size(-2);
2159:   auto n = A.size(-1);
2160:   auto dAh = dA.mH();
2161:   auto pinvAh = pinvA.mH();
2162:   // optimization to produce matrices of the smallest dimension
2163:   if (m <= n) {
2164:     auto K = pinvAh.matmul(dAh);
2165:     return pinvA.matmul(K - K.mH() - K.matmul(A.matmul(pinvA))) +
2166:         (dAh - pinvA.matmul(A.matmul(dAh))).matmul(pinvAh.matmul(pinvA));
2167:   } else {
2168:     auto K = pinvA.matmul(dA);
2169:     auto Kh = K.mH();
2170:     return (Kh - K - pinvA.matmul(A).matmul(Kh)).matmul(pinvA) +
2171:         (pinvA.matmul(pinvAh)).matmul(dAh - (dAh.matmul(A)).matmul(pinvA));
2172:   }
2173: }
```

```cpp
2322: Tensor glu_double_backward(
2323:     const Tensor& grad,
2324:     const Tensor& grad_output,
2325:     const Tensor& input,
2326:     int64_t dim) {
2327:   auto& gO = grad_output;
2328:   auto input_size = input.size(dim) / 2;
2329:   auto first_half = input.narrow(dim, 0, input_size);
2330:   auto second_half = input.narrow(dim, input_size, input_size);
2331:   auto sig_second_half = second_half.sigmoid();
2332:   auto one_sub_sig_second_half = 1 - sig_second_half;
2333:   auto sig_one_sub_sig = sig_second_half * one_sub_sig_second_half;
2334: 
2335:   auto ggI_first_half = grad.narrow(dim, 0, input_size);
2336:   auto ggI_second_half = grad.narrow(dim, input_size, input_size);
2337:   auto ggI_second_half_times_first_half = ggI_second_half * first_half;
2338: 
2339:   auto gI_first_half = ggI_second_half * gO * sig_one_sub_sig;
2340:   auto second_order_sh = sig_one_sub_sig * one_sub_sig_second_half -
2341:       sig_second_half * sig_one_sub_sig;
2342:   auto gI_second_half =
2343:       ggI_second_half_times_first_half * gO * second_order_sh +
2344:       ggI_first_half * gO * sig_one_sub_sig;
2345:   return at::cat({std::move(gI_first_half), std::move(gI_second_half)}, dim);
2346: }
2347: 
2348: Tensor glu_double_backward_grad_output(
2349:     const Tensor& grad,
2350:     const Tensor& input,
2351:     int64_t dim) {
2352:   if (dim < 0)
2353:     dim += input.dim();
2354:   auto sizes = input.sizes().vec();
2355:   sizes[dim] /= 2;
2356:   auto tmp = grad * glu_backward(at::ones(sizes, input.options()), input, dim);
2357:   return tmp.narrow(dim, 0, sizes[dim]) +
2358:       tmp.narrow(dim, sizes[dim], sizes[dim]);
2359: }
```

```cpp
2378: Tensor infinitely_differentiable_logit_backward(
2379:     const Tensor& grad,
2380:     const Tensor& self,
2381:     std::optional<double> eps) {
2382:   if (eps) {
2383:     const double lo = eps.value();
2384:     const double hi = 1.0 - lo;
2385:     return at::where(
2386:         at::logical_and(self >= lo, self <= hi),
2387:         grad / (self * (1.0 - self)),
2388:         at::zeros({}, self.options()));
2389:   } else {
2390:     return at::where(
2391:         at::logical_and(self >= 0.0, self <= 1.0),
2392:         grad / (self * (1.0 - self)),
2393:         at::empty({}, self.options())
2394:             .fill_(std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN()));
2395:   }
2396: }
```

```cpp
2584: // NOTE: [How to write vmap-compatible backward formulas]
2585: //
2586: // See NOTE: [vmap-incompatible in-place operations] for what it means for an
2587: // in-place operation to be incompatible with vmap.
2588: //
2589: // If an in-place operation used in a backward formula is vmap-incompatible,
2590: // then as developers we have the following options:
2591: //
2592: // - If the in-place operation directly followed the creation of a tensor with
2593: //   a factory function like at::zeros(...), we should replace the factory with
2594: //   a corresponding grad.new_zeros(...) call. The grad.new_zeros(...) call
2595: //   propagates the batch dims to the resulting tensor.
2596: //   For example:
2597: //     Before: at::zeros(input.sizes(), grad.options()).copy_(grad)
2598: //     After:  grad.new_zeros(input.sizes()).copy_(grad)
2599: //
2600: // - If the in-place operation followed some sequence of operations, if the
2601: //   we want to be able to vmap over the backward formula as-is (this is
2602: //   usually the case for simple (<15loc) backward formulas), then use
2603: //   areAnyTensorSubclassLike  to guard the operation. For example:
2604: //             c = a * b
2605: //     Before: c.mul_(grad)
2606: //     After:  c = !areAnyTensorSubclassLike({c, grad}) ? c.mul_(grad) : c *
2607: //     grad
2608: //
2609: // - If we don't want to vmap directly over the backward formula (e.g., if the
2610: //   backward formula is too complicated or has a lot of vmap-incompatible
2611: //   operations, then register the backward formula as an operator and
2612: //   eventually write a batching rule for it.
2613:
```

```cpp
2777: // NOTE [ as_strided Backward and layout-aware/agnostic autograd ]
2778: //
2779: // `storage_offset` is ignored for simplicity in this note. If you just want the
2780: // full algorithm without explanation, scroll down to bottom of this note.
2781: //
2782: // Implementing the backward of as_strided is tricky because you have to deal
2783: // with mappings that map one memory location to multiple indices, i.e., the
2784: // output tensor has multiple indices pointing to **overlapping** memory
2785: // addresses. This can happen in all in all sorts of weird cases. For example,
2786: //
2787: //   x = torch.randn(15)
2788: //   x.as_strided([3, 3], [1, 0])  # "expand" case
2789: //   x.as_strided([3, 3], [2, 1])  # "size too large" case
2790: //   x.as_strided([3, 2], [3, 6])  # res[2, 0] points to 2*3 + 0*6 = 6
2791: //                                 # res[0, 1] points to 0*3 + 1*6 = 6
2792: //
2793: // Here is the general strategy we apply in implementing as_strided backward:
2794: //   0. ??? (optimization step. we will talk about this later)
2795: //   1. Create some underlying flattened tensor as if it is the base tensor
2796: //      representing the contiguous memory storage for both input and output.
2797: //   2. Use the output geometry to scatter (or index_add) the gradients into
2798: //      this storage tensor.
2799: //   3. ??? (fix for input tensor with overlapping memory. we will talk about
2800: //           this later)
2801: //   4. Return the as_strided view of the storage tensor using input geometry.
2802: //
2803: // In step (2), if the output tensor doesn't have overlapping memory, we can
2804: // safely scatter (`storage.as_strided(output_geometry).copy_(grad)`);
2805: // otherwise, we must use `index_add` as gradients at different indices may need
2806: // to be summed to a single location.
2807: //
2808: // For example, in this case:
2809: //
2810: //   x = torch.randn(3)
2811: //   y = x.as_strided([3, 3], [1, 0])  # "expand" case
2812: //                                     # size   [ 3, 3]
2813: //                                     # stride [ 1, 0]
2814: //   y.backward()  # step (1): contiguous storagte tensor `s` of size 3, which
2815: //                             is large enough to be used as underlying storage
2816: //                             for `x` and `y`.
```

### Lines 2817-2880

```cpp
2817: //                               s = [ 0, 0, 0]
2818: //                 # step (2): since `y` has overlapping memory, index_add grad
2819: //                             into `s` basing on `y`'s geometry, i.e.,
2820: //                             s[i * y.stride(0) + j * y.stride(1)] += gy[i, j].
2821: //                               s = [ 3, 3, 3]
2822: //                 # step (4): as_strided view `s` using `x`'s geometry
2823: //                               s = [ 3, 3, 3]
2824: //                               grad_input = s.as_strided(x.size(), x.stride())
2825: //                                          = s.as_strided([3], [1])
2826: //                                          = [ 3, 3, 3]
2827: //
2828: // This is exactly what we would get if using `expand`. However, here the input
2829: // tensor doesn't have overlapping memory. If it does, we must add an extra step
2830: // before (4). Considering this case:
2831: //
2832: //   t = torch.randn(3)
2833: //   x = t.expand(3, 3)            # input with overlapping memory
2834: //                                 # size   [3, 3]
2835: //                                 # stride [0, 1]
2836: //   y = x.as_strided([1], [1])    # contiguous output
2837: //                                 # size   [1]
2838: //                                 # stride [1]
2839: //   y.backward()  # step (1): contiguous storage tensor `s` of size 3, which
2840: //                             is large enough to be used as underlying storage
2841: //                             for `x` and `y`.
2842: //                               s = [ 0, 0, 0]
2843: //                 # step (2): scatter grad into `s` basing on `y`'s geometry
2844: //                               s = [ 1, 0, 0]
2845: //                 # step (4): as_strided view `s` using `x`'s geometry
2846: //                               s = [ 1, 0, 0]
2847: //                               grad_input = s.as_strided([3, 3], [0, 1])
2848: //                                          = s.as_strided([3, 3], [0, 1])
2849: //                                          = [[ 1, 0, 0],
2850: //                                             [ 1, 0, 0],
2851: //                                             [ 1, 0, 0]]
2852: // Is this result correct?
2853: //
2854: // `x.as_strided([1], [1])` call is obviously equivalent with
2855: // `x[(0,) * x.dim()].view(1)` for any `x`. But autograd through the second
2856: // gives gradient `[ [ 1, 0, 0], [ 0, 0, 0], [ 0, 0, 0]]`. For this specific
2857: // case, indexing `x` at any index in first column is also equivalent, and
2858: // yields a gradient of shape `[3 x 3]` containing eight 0's and one 1. There is
2859: // an `x.size(1)`-times difference between these gradients computed from other
2860: // PyTorch ops and the gradient we got from as_strided.
2861: //
2862: // You might conclude that the gradients from as_strided is wrong. However,
2863: // let's first see why they are actually reasonable. Consider the pointwise
2864: // perturbations by `delta` anywhere in the first column of `x`. It will lead to
2865: // a `delta` change in the same memory location, and then `y` will change by
2866: // `delta`. So one can say the gradient should be exactly 1 at the first column,
2867: // as given by our above procedure.
2868: //
2869: // In the above computation of numerical gradients, they only match the
2870: // analytical results because strides and memory locations are considered in the
2871: // forward pass, i.e., this op (including both forward and backward) is
2872: // layout-aware.
2873: //
2874: // However, in PyTorch, most (probably all) other ops (forward and backward) are
2875: // layout-agnostic. E.g.,
2876: //
2877: //   t = torch.randn(1)
2878: //   x = t.expand(2)
2879: //   y = x.sum()
2880: //   y.backward()
```

### Lines 2881-2944

```cpp
2881: //
2882: // Layout-agnostic autograd (as it is currently in PyTorch) will give you
2883: //
2884: //   gy = 1
2885: //   gx = [ 1, 1]  # SumBackward:    torch.ones_like(x)
2886: //   gt = [ 2]     # ExpandBackward: gx.sum()
2887: //
2888: // Note that `gx = [ 1, 1]`. However, if you perturb any value in `x` by `delta`
2889: // (the other will also change by `delta`), `y` will change by `2 * delta`. So
2890: // the gradients, if strides are taken into consideration, should be 2.
2891: //
2892: // Layout-aware autograd should give you
2893: //
2894: //   gy = 1
2895: //   gx = [ 2, 2]  # Because the backward considers the fact that the input `x`
2896: //                 # is already expanded.
2897: //   gt = [ 2]     # Layout-aware backward of expand is just a slicing because
2898: //                 # the previous backward should have already taken care of
2899: //                 # strides and made sure that gradients are the same along the
2900: //                 # expanded dimension.
2901: //
2902: // As shown above, these two types are not compatible. Therefore, we must either
2903: // make as_strided layout-agnostic, or make all other ops layout-aware.
2904: //
2905: // It is difficult to support layout-aware autograd (at least in the current
2906: // codebase structure), because it would mean
2907: //   1. storing tensor geometries of every input tensor for backward
2908: //   2. depending on input geometry, the gradient computed from backward change
2909: //   3. ideally enforcing gradient of T to always have same strides as T
2910: // (although these two methods only differ when it comes to overlapping memory)
2911: //
2912: // Therefore, we must formulate `as_strided` in a layout-agnostic way, i.e.,
2913: // giving the same output regardless of the input layout. We consider
2914: // `input.stride()` as a separate independent fixed argument `input_stride`.
2915: // Then, `as_strided(input, size, stride)` can be thought of as:
2916: //   1. "Scatter" each value of `input` into a "storage" using storage location
2917: //      computed from the value's index in `input`, `input.size()` and
2918: //      `input_stride`, but if N values end up in the same location, the value
2919: //      is average of those N values (they will be the same value anyways).
2920: //
2921: //      Formal description:
2922: //        Denote the set of all input indices that pointing to the same storage
2923: //        location `storage[n]` as `S(n)`, i.e.,
2924: //
2925: //            S(n) = { index : <index, input_stride> == n, index is valid given
2926: //            input.size() },
2927: //
2928: //        where `<x, y>` is the dot product between `x` and `y`.
2929: //
2930: //        Then, the process is:
2931: //
2932: //            storage[n] = Avg { S(n) }
2933: //
2934: //        Note that all values in `S(n)` are the same (they point to the same
2935: //        memory location anyways, so this step doesn't change anything, but
2936: //        effectively avoids having the dependency on the layout of `input`.
2937: //        I.e., the result holds fixed regardless of the layout of `input`, as
2938: //        long as `input_stride` is fixed.
2939: //
2940: //      NOTE: for forward pass, we can equivalently simply select any one of
2941: //            `S(n)` as `storage[n]`. However, considering this as an average
2942: //            operation makes backward easier (so all values in set
2943: //            `{ grad_input[i] : i in S(n) }` are the same, and it can use the
2944: //            same geometry as input).
```

### Lines 2945-3008

```cpp
2945: //   2. As usual, return the as_strided view of `storage` using required output
2946: //      `size` and `stride`.
2947: //
2948: // To backward through this layout-agnostic version, we simply add the following
2949: // step:
2950: //   .... (scatter gradients into the storage tensor using output geometry)
2951: //   3. For all storage location n, `storage[n] /= |S(n)|`.
2952: //   .... (return as_strided view of the storage tensor using input geometry)
2953: //
2954: // Finally, we note that these general operations are expensive, so we apply the
2955: // following optimizations:
2956: //   Add step (0): For all output dimension `d` with output stride 0, sum the
2957: //                 gradients along dimension `d` (don't keepdim), and remove
2958: //                 dimension `d` from output size and stride.
2959: //                 (An optimization for "expand" cases so we may avoid step (3))
2960: //  Only apply step (3) when input tensor has overlapping memory.
2961: //
2962: // FULL ALGORITHM:
2963: //   0. For all output dimension `d` with output stride 0, sum the gradients
2964: //       along dimension `d` (don't keepdim), and remove dimension `d` from
2965: //       output size and stride.
2966: //   1. Create some underlying flattened tensor as if it is the base tensor
2967: //      representing the contiguous memory storage for both input and output.
2968: //   2. Use the output geometry to scatter (or index_add) the gradients into
2969: //      this storage tensor `storage`.
2970: //   3. If input tensor has overlapping memory,
2971: //      For all storage location `i`, `storage[i] /= N(i)`, where `N(i)` is the
2972: //      number of indices in input geometry pointing to the same storage
2973: //      location `i` (i.e., `|S(i)|` in equations above).
2974: //   4. Return the as_strided view of the storage tensor using input geometry.
2975: //
2976: // See NOTE [ Detecting Memory Overlap Within A Strided Tensor ] on how to
2977: // roughly detect overlapping memory.
2978: 
2979: // NOTE [ Detecting Memory Overlap Within A Strided Tensor ]
2980: //
2981: // Checking memory overlap within a strided tensor is the special case of
2982: // detecting memory overlap of two strided tensors, where the two tensors start
2983: // at the same memory address. The later is HARD (see #8212).
2984: //
2985: // But even this special case isn't simple. This note describes a check for a
2986: // even more constrained simple case where we can be certain that there is no
2987: // overlap.
2988: //
2989: // The checking algorithm can be described as:
2990: //   0. Return [ pass check ] if any dimension has size 0
2991: //   1. Ignore all dimensions that have size 1
2992: //   2. If no remaining dimensions, return [ pass check ]
2993: //   3. Sort the remaining dimensions according to the strides decreasingly
2994: //   4. Check that for each dimension k,
2995: //
2996: //           stride[k] > \sum_{ i > k } (size[i] - 1) * stride[i]
2997: //
2998: //      That is equivalent to, after reordering the dimensions so strides are
2999: //      in decreasing order, checking that stride of each dimension is larger
3000: //      than the maximum memory offset in a slice at that dimension.
3001: //
3002: // Obviously this check passes for contiguous tensors ( the dimensions will be
3003: // already sorted with LHS = stride[0] = \prod size[i] being exactly 1 larger
3004: // than RHS ). Similarly, the check passes for tensors contiguous in all but
3005: // the last dimension, and LHS = stride[0] = stride[-1] * \prod size[i] being
3006: // exactly stride[-1] larger than RHS. (*)
3007: //
3008: // We will show that these view operations, including all our view operations
```

```cpp
3366: Tensor as_strided_scatter_backward(
3367:     const Tensor& grad,
3368:     const TensorGeometry& input_geometry,
3369:     const TensorGeometry& src_geometry,
3370:     c10::SymIntArrayRef sizes,
3371:     c10::SymIntArrayRef strides,
3372:     std::optional<c10::SymInt> storage_offset) {
3373:   // Note [as_strided_scatter backward support]
3374:   // as_strided_scatter handling for autograd is a beast, and is non-trivial to
3375:   // implement for arbitrarily strided inputs. Most uses for as_strided with
3376:   // functionalization only care about the contiguous case anyway, So for now
3377:   // this is not implemented. When autograd is being used, we ban non-contiguous
3378:   // inputs. We can assume that the input was a contiguous tensor. Also, we'll
3379:   // take the perf hit and contiguify grad for now.
3380:   auto grad_ = grad.contiguous();
3381:   auto grad_slice = grad_.as_strided_symint(sizes, strides, storage_offset);
3382:   auto result_buffer = grad_.new_zeros_symint(input_geometry.sym_sizes());
3383:   auto result = result_buffer.as_strided_symint(
3384:       input_geometry.sym_sizes(), input_geometry.sym_strides());
3385:   auto result_slice = result_buffer.as_strided_symint(
3386:       sizes, strides, std::move(storage_offset));
3387:   result_slice.copy_(grad_slice);
3388:   return result;
3389: }
```

```cpp
3412: Tensor gelu_double_backward(
3413:     const Tensor& ggI,
3414:     const Tensor& gO,
3415:     const Tensor& input,
3416:     std::string_view approximate) {
3417:   // if (at::native::get_gelutype_enum(approximate) ==
3418:   // at::native::GeluType::Tanh) {
3419:   if (approximate == "tanh") {
3420:     constexpr auto kBeta = M_SQRT2 * M_2_SQRTPI * 0.5;
3421:     constexpr auto kKappa = 0.044715;
3422: 
3423:     auto inner = kBeta * (input + kKappa * pow(input, 3));
3424:     auto tanh_inner = tanh(inner);
3425:     auto sech_inner = 1 / cosh(inner);
3426: 
3427:     auto f = 0.5 * input;
3428:     auto g = 1 - tanh_inner * tanh_inner;
3429:     auto h = kBeta * (1 + 3 * kKappa * input * input);
3430: 
3431:     auto f_prime_gh = 0.5 * g * h;
3432: 
3433:     auto g_prime = (2 * sech_inner) * (-sech_inner * tanh_inner) * h;
3434:     auto g_prime_fh = f * h * g_prime;
3435: 
3436:     auto h_prime = 6 * kKappa * input * kBeta;
3437:     auto h_prime_fg = f * g * h_prime;
3438: 
3439:     // left_derivative = f_prime_gh
3440:     // right_derivative = f_prime_gh + g_prime_fh + h_prime_fg
3441:     // dgrad_dX = left_derivative + right_derivative
3442:     auto gI = ggI * gO * (2 * f_prime_gh + g_prime_fh + h_prime_fg);
3443:     return gI;
3444:   } else {
3445:     constexpr auto kBeta = M_2_SQRTPI * M_SQRT1_2 * 0.5;
3446:     auto input_sq = input * input;
3447:     auto pdf = kBeta * at::exp(-0.5 * input_sq);
3448:     auto dgrad_dInput = 2 * pdf - input_sq * pdf;
3449:     auto gI = ggI * gO * dgrad_dInput;
3450:     return gI;
3451:   }
3452: }
```

```cpp
3605: Tensor svd_backward(
3606:     const Tensor& gU,
3607:     const Tensor& gS,
3608:     const Tensor& gVh,
3609:     const Tensor& U,
3610:     const Tensor& S,
3611:     const Tensor& Vh) {
3612:   at::NoTF32Guard disable_tf32;
3613:   // Throughout both the real and complex case we assume A has distinct singular
3614:   // values. Furthermore, if A is rectangular or complex, we assume it's
3615:   // full-rank.
3616:   //
3617:   //
3618:   // The real case (A \in R)
3619:   // See e.g. https://j-towns.github.io/papers/svd-derivative.pdf
3620:   //
3621:   // Denote by skew(X) = X - X^T, and by A o B the coordinatewise product, then
3622:   // if m == n
3623:   //   gA = U [(skew(U^T gU) / E)S + S(skew(V^T gV) / E) + I o gS ]V^T
3624:   // where E_{jk} = S_k^2 - S_j^2 if j != k and 1 otherwise
3625:   //
3626:   // if m > n
3627:   //   gA = [term in m == n] + (I_m - UU^T)gU S^{-1} V^T
3628:   // if m < n
3629:   //   gA = [term in m == n] + U S^{-1} (gV)^T (I_n - VV^T)
3630:   //
3631:   //
3632:   // The complex case (A \in C)
3633:   // This one is trickier because the svd is not locally unique.
3634:   // Denote L = diag(e^{i\theta_k}), then we have that if A = USV^H, then (UL,
3635:   // S, VL) is another valid SVD decomposition of A as A = ULS(VL)^H =
3636:   // ULSL^{-1}V^H = USV^H, since L, S and L^{-1} commute, since they are all
3637:   // diagonal.
3638:   //
3639:   // Assume wlog that n >= k in what follows, as otherwise we could reason about
3640:   // A^H. Denote by St_k(C^n) = {A \in C^{n,k} | A^H A = I_k} the complex
3641:   // Stiefel manifold. What this invariance means is that the svd decomposition
3642:   // is not a map svd: C^{n x k} -> St_k(C^n) x R^n x St_k(C^k) (where St_k(C^k)
3643:   // is simply the unitary group U(k)) but a map svd: C^{n x k} -> M x R^n where
3644:   // M is the manifold given by quotienting St_k(C^n) x U(n) by the action (U,
3645:   // V) -> (UL, VL) with L as above. Note that M is a manifold, because the
3646:   // action is free and proper (as U(1)^k \iso (S^1)^k is compact). For this
3647:   // reason, pi : St_k(C^n) x U(n) -> M forms a principal bundle.
3648:   //
```

```cpp
3649:   // To think about M, consider the case case k = 1. The, we have the bundle
3650:   // pi : St_1(C^n) x U(1) -> M
3651:   // now, St_1(C^n) are just vectors of norm 1 in C^n. That's exactly the sphere
3652:   // of dimension 2n-1 in C^n \iso R^{2n} S^{2n-1} = { z \in C^n | z^H z = 1}.
3653:   // Then, in this case, we're quotienting out U(1) completely, so we get that
3654:   // pi : S^{2n-1} x U(1) -> CP(n-1)
3655:   // where CP(n-1) is the complex projective space of dimension n-1.
3656:   // In other words, M is just the complex projective space, and pi is (pretty
3657:   // similar to) the usual principal bundle from S^{2n-1} to CP(n-1). The case k
3658:   // > 1 is the same, but requiring a linear independence condition between the
3659:   // vectors from the different S^{2n-1} or CP(n-1).
3660:   //
3661:   // Note that this is a U(1)^k-bundle. In plain words, this means that the
3662:   // fibres of this bundle, i.e. pi^{-1}(x) for x \in M are isomorphic to U(1) x
3663:   // ... x U(1). This is obvious as, if pi(U,V) = x, pi^{-1}(x) = {(U
3664:   // diag(e^{i\theta}), V diag(e^{i\theta})) | \theta \in R^k}
3665:   //            = {(U diag(z), V diag(z)) | z \in U(1)^k}
3666:   // since U(1) = {z \in C | |z| = 1}.
3667:   //
3668:   // The big issue here is that M with its induced metric is not locally
3669:   // isometric to St_k(C^n) x U(k). [The why is rather technical, but you can
3670:   // see that the horizontal distribution is not involutive, and hence
3671:   // integrable due to Frobenius' theorem] What this means in plain words is
3672:   // that, no matter how we choose to return the U and V from the SVD, we won't
3673:   // be able to simply differentiate wrt. U and V and call it a day. An example
3674:   // of a case where we can do this is when performing an eigendecomposition on
3675:   // a real matrix that happens to have real eigendecomposition. In this case,
3676:   // even though you can rescale the eigenvectors by any real number, you can
3677:   // choose them of norm 1 and call it a day. In the eigenvector case, we are
3678:   // using that you can isometrically embed S^{n-1} into R^n. In the svd case,
3679:   // we need to work with the "quotient manifold" M explicitly, which is
3680:   // slightly more technically challenging.
3681:   //
3682:   // Since the columns of U and V are not uniquely defined, but are
3683:   // representatives of certain classes of equivalence which represent elements
3684:   // M, the user may not depend on the particular representative that we return
3685:   // from the SVD. In particular, if the loss function depends on U or V, it
3686:   // must be invariant under the transformation (U, V) -> (UL, VL) with L =
3687:   // diag(e^{i\theta})), for every \theta \in R^k. In more geometrical terms,
3688:   // this means that the loss function should be constant on the fibres, or, in
3689:   // other words, the gradient along the fibres should be zero. We may see this
3690:   // by checking that the gradients as element in the tangent space T_{(U,
3691:   // V)}(St(n,k) x U(k)) are normal to the fibres. Differentiating the map (U,
3692:   // V) -> (UL, VL), we see that the space tangent to the fibres is given by
3693:   // Vert_{(U, V)}(St(n,k) x U(k)) = { i[U, V]diag(\theta) | \theta in R^k}
3694:   // where [U, V] denotes the vertical concatenation of U and V to form an (n+k,
3695:   // k) matrix. Then, solving <i[U,V]diag(\theta), [S, T]> = 0 for two matrices
3696:   // S, T \in T_{(U, V)}(St(n,k) x U(k)) where <A, B> = Re tr(A^H B) is the
3697:   // canonical (real) inner product in C^{n x k} we get that the function is

3699:   //
3700:   // Using this in the derviaton for the forward AD, one sees that, with the
3701:   // notation from those notes Using this and writing sym(X) = X + X^H, we get
3702:   // that the forward AD for SVD in the complex case is given by dU = U (sym(dX
3703:   // S) / E + i Im(diag(dX)) / (2S)) if m > n
```

```cpp
3923: std::tuple<Tensor, Tensor> linalg_eig_jvp(
3924:     const Tensor& dA,
3925:     const Tensor& L,
3926:     const Tensor& V,
3927:     const bool is_hermitian) {
3928:   at::NoTF32Guard disable_tf32;
3929:   // https://people.maths.ox.ac.uk/gilesm/files/NA-08-01.pdf
3930:   // see also https://arxiv.org/pdf/1701.00392.pdf Eqs. (4.60) and (4.63)
3931:   // Note that neither of the formulas in these pdfs are correct, as they do not
3932:   // assume that the eigenvectors are of unit norm. As such, they are missing
3933:   // the diagonal term in dV dL = diag(dP) dV = dX - V Re(diag V^H dX)) where dP
3934:   // = V^{-1} dA V dX = V ((dP - diag(dP)) / E) E_{ij} = L_j - L_i if i != j
3935:   //          1         otherwise
3936: 
3937:   // Precondition: if is_hermitian == true, then dA is Hermitian
3938:   const auto to_complex = [](const Tensor& A) {
3939:     return A.to(c10::toComplexType(A.scalar_type()));
3940:   };
3941: 
3942:   const auto dP = is_hermitian
3943:       ? at::matmul(at::matmul(V.mH(), dA), V)
3944:       : at::linalg_solve(V, at::matmul(to_complex(dA), V));
3945:   auto dL = is_hermitian && dA.is_complex() ? at::real(dP.diagonal(0, -2, -1))
3946:                                             : dP.diagonal(0, -2, -1);
3947:   auto dV = [&dP, &V, &L, is_hermitian] {
3948:     auto dX = [&] {
3949:       auto ret = dP / (L.unsqueeze(-2) - L.unsqueeze(-1));
3950:       ret.diagonal(0, -2, -1).zero_();
3951:       ret = at::matmul(V, ret);
3952:       return ret;
3953:     }();
3954: 
3955:     if (is_hermitian) {
3956:       return dX;
3957:     } else {
3958:       return dX -
3959:           V *
3960:           at::real(at::matmul(V.mH(), dX).diagonal(0, -2, -1)).unsqueeze(-2);
3961:     }
3962:   }();
3963:   return std::make_pair(std::move(dL), std::move(dV));
3964: }
```

```cpp
4008:   auto dL = 2 * at::real(r * dr.conj()).sum(-2);
```

```cpp
4319: template <typename F1, typename F2, typename... Ts>
4320: static Tensor masked_fmap(
4321:     const Tensor& mask,
4322:     const F1& f1,
4323:     const F2& f2,
4324:     const Tensor& t,
4325:     const Ts&... ts) {
4326:   // This function takes two functions f1 and f2 and a (variadic) list of
4327:   // tensors, and creates a new tensor of the same shape as the first element of
4328:   // the list of tensors by applying the function f1 to the tensors for which
4329:   // the mask is true and f2 to the tensors for which the mask is false This
4330:   // function is used when we have a formula that works for, say, all
4331:   // non-singular inputs and another one for when the inputs are singular. See
4332:   // for example det_backward
4333: 
4334:   // Precondition for the n == 0 case to make sense
4335:   TORCH_INTERNAL_ASSERT(t.sym_numel() != 0);
4336:   auto t_masked = t.index({mask});
4337:   auto n = t_masked.sym_numel();
4338:   if (n == t.sym_numel()) {
4339:     return f1(t, ts...);
4340:   } else if (n == 0) {
4341:     return f2(t, ts...);
4342:   } else {
4343:     // Equivalent to
4344:     // ret = torch.empty_like(t)
4345:     // ret[mask] = f1(t1[mask], ..., tn[mask])
4346:     // ret[~mask] = f2(t1[~mask], ..., tn[~mask])
4347:     auto not_mask = mask.logical_not();
4348:     return at::empty_like(t)
4349:         .index_put_({mask}, f1(t_masked, ts.index({mask})...))
4350:         .index_put_(
4351:             {not_mask}, f2(t.index({not_mask}), ts.index({not_mask})...));
4352:   }
```

```cpp
4616: std::tuple<Tensor, Tensor> linalg_solve_triangular_backward(
4617:     const Tensor& grad,
4618:     const Tensor& A,
4619:     const Tensor& X,
4620:     const bool upper,
4621:     const bool left,
4622:     const bool unitriangular,
4623:     std::array<bool, 2> output_mask) {
4624:   at::NoTF32Guard disable_tf32;
4625:   const bool A_requires_grad = output_mask[0];
4626:   const bool B_requires_grad = output_mask[1];
4627:   // [Note: Forward / Backward AD solve_triangular]
4628:   // Assume left=true for simplicity.
4629:   // Remark: A solver computes A^{-1}B
4630:   //
4631:   // Forward AD:
4632:   // If f(A) = A^{-1}, differentiating the equation A^{-1}A = I_n gives
4633:   // (df)_A(E) = -A^{-1}EA^{-1}
4634:   // As such, if g(A,B) = A^{-1}B,
4635:   // (dg)_(A,B)(E_A, E_B) = -A^{-1}E_AA^{-1}B + A^{-1}E_B
4636:   //                      = A^{-1}(E_B - E_AX)
4637: 
4638:   // Backward AD:
4639:   // Denoting the gradients by G_A, G_B, we solve above to give
4640:   // G_B = A^{-H}G_X
4641:   // G_A = -A^{-H}G_XX^H = -G_B X^H
4642:   //
4643:   // Note that you don't need to store B for forward nor backward
4644:   //
4645:   // These formulas work for a general solver of linear equations.
4646:   // Let's prove now that when A is triangular, G_A is the projection onto the
4647:   // triangular matrices of the formula above, i.e. simply taking triu (resp.
4648:   // tril) in the formula above. This is because, since the triangular matrices
4649:   // form a vector space, the tangent space at any point is itself the space of
4650:   // triangular matrices. The result follows from a reasoning as that at the end
4651:   // of [Note: eigh backward] Something similar happens for `unitriangular`,
4652:   // only that int his case the tangent space is the set of lower-triangular
4653:   // matrices with zeros on the diagonal.
4654: 
4655:   if (!grad.defined() || (!A_requires_grad && !B_requires_grad)) {
4656:     return std::make_tuple(Tensor{}, Tensor{});
4657:   }
4658:   // We always need to compute G_B
4659:   const Tensor A_H = A.mH();
4660:   const Tensor G_B =
4661:       at::linalg_solve_triangular(A_H, grad, !upper, left, unitriangular);
4662: 
4663:   if (A_requires_grad) {
4664:     const Tensor X_H = X.mH();
4665:     Tensor G_A = left ? -at::matmul(G_B, X_H) : -at::matmul(X_H, G_B);
4666:     G_A = upper ? G_A.triu(static_cast<int>(unitriangular))
4667:                 : G_A.tril(-static_cast<int>(unitriangular));
4668:     return std::make_tuple(std::move(G_A), B_requires_grad ? G_B : Tensor{});
4669:   } else {
4670:     return std::make_tuple(Tensor{}, G_B);
4671:   }
4672: }
```

```cpp
4719: Tensor fft_c2r_backward(
4720:     const Tensor& grad,
4721:     IntArrayRef dim,
4722:     int64_t normalization) {
4723:   // Forward is C2R (onesided)
4724:   // Think of onesided C2R irfft as
4725:   //    1. fill the other half by conjugate symmetry
4726:   //    2. inverse C2C ifft
4727:   //    3. discard the complex dimension
4728:   // So backward is
4729:   //    1. R2C rfft (essentially add dummy complex dimension, and dft)
4730:   //    2. accumulate gradient by conjugate symmetry
4731:   //       since rfft results follow conjugate symmetry, we only need to
4732:   //       double some entries from onesided rfft results, i.e., the ones with
4733:   //       their reflected indices also landing out of the onesided range. So
4734:   //       consider the index of last dim:
4735:   //           i.   idx = 0.
4736:   //                Reflected to (N - 0) % N = 0. Not doubled.
```

```cpp
4742:   //           iv.  idx = floor(N/2) = (N-1)/2 (last) when N odd.

4759: Tensor fft_r2c_backward(
4760:     const Tensor& grad,
4761:     at::IntArrayRef dim,
4762:     int64_t normalization,
4763:     bool onesided,
4764:     const c10::SymInt& last_dim_size) {
4765:   if (!onesided) {
4766:     return at::real(at::_fft_c2c(grad, dim, normalization, /*forward=*/false));
4767:   }
4768: 
4769:   // Forward is R2C (onesided)
4770:   // Think of onesided R2C rfft as
4771:   //     1. view as complex numbers (fill complex dim with zeros)
4772:   //     2. C2C fft
4773:   //     3. discard half of results
4774:   // So backward is
4775:   //     1. fill the other half with zeros (with `zero_grad_shape` below)
4776:   //        (C2C ifft only take twosided inputs so we need to fill here)
4777:   //     2. inverse C2C ifft
4778:   //     3. discard the complex dim
4779:   auto half_sizes = grad.sym_sizes();
4780:   std::vector<c10::SymInt> new_grad_shape(half_sizes.begin(), half_sizes.end());
4781:   const auto last_dim =
4782:       at::maybe_wrap_dim(dim.back(), static_cast<int64_t>(half_sizes.size()));
4783:   new_grad_shape[last_dim] = last_dim_size;
4784: 
4785:   const auto zero_length = last_dim_size - grad.sym_size(dim.back());
4786:   auto complex_full_grad =
4787:       zero_length > 0 ? grad.new_zeros_symint(new_grad_shape) : grad;
4788:   if (zero_length > 0) {
4789:     complex_full_grad.slice_symint(last_dim, 0, half_sizes[last_dim])
4790:         .copy_(grad);
4791:   }
4792:   return at::real(
4793:       at::_fft_c2c(complex_full_grad, dim, normalization, /*forward=*/false));
4794: }
```

```cpp
5333:   if (self.layout() == c10::kSparse || self.layout() == c10::kSparseCsr ||
5334:       self.layout() == c10::kSparseCsc || self.layout() == c10::kSparseBsr ||
5335:       self.layout() == c10::kSparseBsc) {
5336:     // The warning only applies to the sparsity of self, dense grad is never
5337:     // materialized so if self is strided and grad is sparse nothing unexpected
5338:     // happens memory wise
5339:     TORCH_WARN(
5340:         "log1p_backward: received self with sparse layout, but backward requires materialization of a dense tensor with this shape");
5341:     self_p1_conj = (self.to_dense() + 1).conj();
5342:   } else {
5343:     // Although calling self.to_dense() would just return self when it has
5344:     // strided layout, that would breaks functorch tests.
5345:     self_p1_conj = (self + 1).conj();
5346:   }
5347:   if (grad.layout() == c10::kSparse || grad.layout() == c10::kSparseCsr ||
5348:       grad.layout() == c10::kSparseCsc || grad.layout() == c10::kSparseBsr ||
5349:       grad.layout() == c10::kSparseBsc) {
5350:     // If grad is sparse we can't divide by the n-d (self + 1).conj(), so we
5351:     // must multiply by the recipricol, layout of grad is preserved which is
5352:     // important to gradcheck
5353:     return grad * self_p1_conj.reciprocal_();
5354:   }
5355:   return grad / self_p1_conj;
5356: }

5358: Tensor sinc_backward(const Tensor& grad, const Tensor& self) {
5359:   auto self_pi = self * M_PI;
5360:   auto self_squared_pi = self * self * M_PI;
5361:   auto out = grad *
5362:       ((self_pi * self_pi.cos() - self_pi.sin()) / self_squared_pi).conj();
5363:   return at::where(
5364:       self_squared_pi == 0.0, at::scalar_tensor(0.0, grad.options()), out);
5365: }
```

```cpp
5441: // Derivations for the householder_product.backward method.
5442: //
5443: // Given a sequence of vectors v_1, ..., v_n and a sequence of scalars tau_1,
5444: // ..., tau_k, the torch.linalg.householder_product computes the first n columns
5445: // of the following product: Q = (I - tau_1 v_1 v_1^H) ... (I - tau_k v_k
5446: // v_k^H). Let
5447: //     H_i(sigma) := I - sigma v_i v_i^H, so Q = (H_1(sigma_1) ...
5448: //     H_k(sigma_k)[:, :k]; H_i_minus = H_1(tau_1) ... H_{i - 1}(tau_{i - 1}),
5449: //     with H_1_minus := I; H_i_plus = H_{i + 1}(tau_{i + 1}) ... H_k(tau_k)
5450: //     with H_k_plus := I;
5451: //
5452: // Forward AD:
5453: // dQ = sum_{i = 1}^k H_i_minus (-dtau_i v_i v_i^H - tau_i dv_i v_i^H - tau_i
5454: // v_i dv_i^H) H_i_plus.
5455: //
5456: // Backward AD:
5457: // Tr(Q_grad^H dQ) = sum_{i = 1}^k Tr(H_i_plus Q_grad^H H_i_minus (-dtau_i v_i
5458: // v_i^H - tau_i dv_i v_i^H - tau_i v_i dv_i^H)). Let K_i := H_i_plus Q_grad^H
5459: // H_i_minus, then the gradients are v_i_grad = (-tau_i v_i^H K_i)^H - tau_i K_i
5460: // v_i, tau_i_grad = Tr(-v_i^H K_i v_i).conj(). NOTE: the algorithms ignores
5461: // that only n columns of Q are observed, so there is no need in recomputing Q
5462: // to full completion.
5463: //
5464: // Note that K_{i + 1} = H_{i + 1}^{-1} K_i H_i, so we can compute v_i_grad,
5465: // tau_i_grad one by one by just efficiently updating K_i if that is possible.
5466: // Multiplying with H_i from the right could be done with matrix-vector
5467: // products, but what about the inverse H_{i + 1}^{-1} and does it even exist?
5468: // Luckily, under some assumptions, H_{i + 1}^{-1} exists and admits a
5469: // representation as H_i(sigma_i) for some sigma_i, so the left update is also
5470: // could be done with matrix-vector and not matrix-matrix products.
5471: //
5472: // Let H(tau) := I - tau v v^H.
5473: // H(tau) has eigenvalues 1 with multiplicity (m - 1) with eigenvectors
5474: // orthogonal to v, and an eigenvalue (1 - tau ||v||^2) with the corresponding
5475: // eigenvector v / ||v||. If (1 - tau ||v||^2) != 0, H(tau) is invertible. If (1
5476: // - tau ||v||^2) != 0, then with sigma defined as sigma := tau / (||v||^2 tau -
5477: // 1) we get that H(tau) H(sigma) = H(sigma) H(tau) = I, so H(sigma) is the
5478: // inverse of H(tau).
5479: //
5480: // WARNING: the algorithm below assumes that H_i(tau_i) are all invertible, so
5481: // it expects that (1 - tau_i ||v_i||^2) != 0 for all i.
5482: // We would like to point out that if there is H_i(tau_i) which is not
5483: // invertible, the householder_product is still differentiable! We will not be
5484: // able to compute K_i efficiently in such cases, however, as evaluating of each
5485: // K_i will amount to calls to ORGQR to be able to compute H_i_plus.
5486: 
5487: // This function computes either the product between
5488: // (I - tau u v^H) and K (in-place or not) with `condition_with_I = true`, or
5489: // between
5490: // (-tau u v^H) and K (out-of-place only) with `condition_with_I = false`.
5491: // Parameter `left` controls whether the matrix K is multiplied from the left or
5492: // from the right.
5493: // Additionally, when the computation is done in-place, we exploit that the
5494: // first `k` coordinates of `u_full/v_full` are zeros.
5495: static Tensor apply_simple_transformation(
5496:     const c10::SymInt& m,
5497:     const c10::SymInt& k,
5498:     const Tensor& u_full,
5499:     const Tensor& v_full,
5500:     const Tensor& t,
5501:     Tensor& K,
5502:     bool modify_K_in_place = true,
5503:     bool condition_with_I = true,
5504:     bool left = true) {
```

```cpp
5544: std::tuple<Tensor, Tensor> householder_product_backward(
5545:     const Tensor& grad,
5546:     const Tensor& result,
5547:     const Tensor& input_,
5548:     const Tensor& tau,
5549:     const bool flip_order) {
5550:   // NOTE on `flip_order`: when flip_order is true,
5551:   // the algorithm below reverses the processing direction from
5552:   // range(k) to range(k - 1, -1, -1) in the main loop, and left/right
5553:   // Householder projection applications get flipped.
5554:   // The comments below about the algorithmic details assume flip_order = false.
5555:   if (!grad.defined() || input_.sym_numel() == 0 || tau.sym_numel() == 0) {
5556:     return std::tuple<Tensor, Tensor>(Tensor(), Tensor());
5557:   }
5558:   auto m = input_.sym_size(-2);
5559:   // guard_int is due to irange calls below
5560:   auto k = tau.sym_size(-1).guard_int(__FILE__, __LINE__);
5561: 
5562:   // forward operates only over the lower triangular part with the assumption
5563:   // that the diagonal of input is filled with 1s.
5564:   auto input = input_.tril(-1);
5565:   input.diagonal(0, -2, -1).fill_(1.0);
5566: 
5567:   // compute sigma such that
5568:   // H(sigma_i) == H(tau_i)^{-1}.
```

```cpp
5569:   // If the input to householder_product comes from GEQRF,
5570:   // we will never encounter ||v_i||^2 tau_i == 1, so H(tau_i) will always be
5571:   // invertible. This follows from the documentation
5572:   // https://www.netlib.org/lapack/lug/node128.html, and tau always satisfying
5573:   // the condition |tau|^2 ||v||^2 == 2 * Re(tau).
5574:   auto input_first_k_cols = input.narrow(-1, 0, k);
5575:   auto input_first_k_cols_norm_squared =
5576:       (input_first_k_cols * input_first_k_cols.conj()).sum(-2);
5577:   auto sigma = tau / (tau * input_first_k_cols_norm_squared - 1.0);
5578: 
5579:   auto K = result.matmul(grad.mH());
5580: 
5581:   // The algorithm updates K by multiplying it from the left/right with
5582:   // Householder reflectors. If only single backward is run, we modify K
5583:   // in-place and exploit triangularity of the input. With higher order
5584:   // derivatives we cannot rewrite the storage of K, hence we use much less
5585:   // efficient out-of-place methods.
5586:   //
5587:   // if only first-order derivative is expected, we can modify K in-place for
5588:   // better performance
5589:   bool modify_K_in_place = !at::GradMode::is_enabled();
5590: 
5591:   // This method exploits that at k-th iteration vector v_k has only elements
5592:   // v_k[k:] which are non-zero.
5593:   auto update_grad = [&m](
5594:                          int64_t k,
5595:                          const Tensor& v_full,
5596:                          const Tensor& t,
5597:                          const Tensor& K) -> std::tuple<Tensor, Tensor> {
5598:     // v_full is a vector of dimension (..., m, 1), t is a scalar of dimension
5599:     // (..., 1)
5600:     auto v = v_full.narrow_symint(-2, k, m - k);
5601:     auto vHK = v.mH().matmul(K.narrow_symint(-2, k, m - k));
5602:     auto Kv = K.narrow_symint(-1, k, m - k).matmul(v);
5603:     auto t_unsqueezed = t.unsqueeze(-1);
5604:     auto v_grad = (-t_unsqueezed * vHK).conj().squeeze(-2) -
5605:         (t_unsqueezed * Kv).squeeze(-1);
5606:     auto tau_grad = -(vHK.narrow_symint(-1, k, m - k).matmul(v)).conj();
5607:     return std::make_tuple(v_grad.unsqueeze(-1), tau_grad.squeeze(-1));
5608:   };
5609:

5614:                                          Tensor& K,
5615:                                          bool left = true) -> Tensor {
5616:     return apply_simple_transformation(
```

```cpp
5722: Tensor householder_product_jvp(
5723:     const Tensor& dV_,
5724:     const Tensor& dtau,
5725:     const Tensor& prod,
5726:     const Tensor& V_,
5727:     const Tensor& tau) {
5728:   auto m = V_.sym_size(-2);
5729:   auto k = tau.size(-1);
5730: 
5731:   // forward operates only over the lower triangular part with the assumption
5732:   // that the diagonal of input is filled with 1s.
5733:   auto V = V_.tril(-1);
5734:   V.diagonal(0, -2, -1).fill_(1.0);
5735:   auto dV = dV_.tril(-1);
5736: 
5737:   // compute sigma such that
5738:   // H(sigma_i) == H(tau_i)^{-1}.
5739:   // If the input to householder_product comes from GEQRF,
5740:   // we will never encounter ||v_i||^2 tau_i == 1, so H(tau_i) will always be
5741:   // invertible. This follows from the documentation
5742:   // https://www.netlib.org/lapack/lug/node128.html, and tau always satisfying
5743:   // the condition |tau|^2 ||v||^2 == 2 * Re(tau).
5744:   auto V_first_k_cols = V.narrow(-1, 0, k);
5745:   auto V_first_k_cols_norm_squared =
5746:       (V_first_k_cols * V_first_k_cols.conj()).sum(-2);
5747:   auto sigma = tau / (tau * V_first_k_cols_norm_squared - 1.0);
5748: 
5749:   auto apply_householder_reflector = [m](const Tensor& v_full,
5750:                                          const Tensor& t,
5751:                                          Tensor& K,
5752:                                          bool left = true) -> Tensor {
5753:     return apply_simple_transformation(
5754:         // setting `modify_K_in_place = true` causes CUDA memory leaks in OpInfo
5755:         // tests of forward AD for that reason we ignore `k` by passing zero
5756:         m,
5757:         /*k=*/0,
5758:         v_full,
5759:         v_full,
5760:         t,
```

```cpp
5895: Tensor i1_backward(
5896:     const Tensor& grad,
5897:     const Tensor& self,
5898:     const Tensor& result) {
5899:   return AT_DISPATCH_FLOATING_TYPES(self.scalar_type(), "i1_backward", [&]() {
5900:     // For x = 0, the correct gradient is 0.5,
5901:     // however due to floating point computation we get NaN.
5902:     // So we manually update gradient for x=0
5903:     auto eps = std::numeric_limits<scalar_t>::epsilon();
5904:     auto self_is_not_tiny = self.abs() > eps;
5905: 
5906:     // Following `where` is needed as `where` computes gradients,
5907:     // even for the part which didn't affect the output.
5908:     // Look at https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/52248
5909:     // Update if and when this is fixed.
5910:     auto safe_self = at::where(
5911:         self_is_not_tiny, self, at::scalar_tensor(eps, self.options()));
5912:     auto gradx = (safe_self.i0() - (result * safe_self.reciprocal()));
5913:     return grad *
5914:         at::where(
5915:                self_is_not_tiny, gradx, at::scalar_tensor(0.5, self.options()));
5916:   });
5917: }

5924:     // For x = 0, the correct gradient is 0.5,
5925:     // however due to floating point computation we get NaN.
5926:     // So we manually update gradient for x=0
5927:     auto eps = std::numeric_limits<scalar_t>::epsilon();
5928:     auto self_is_not_tiny = self.abs() > eps;
5929: 
5930:     // Following `where` is needed as `where` computes gradients,
5931:     // even for the part which didn't affect the output.
5932:     // Look at https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/52248
5933:     // Update if and when this is fixed.
5934:     auto safe_self = at::where(
5935:         self_is_not_tiny, self, at::scalar_tensor(eps, self.options()));
5936:     auto gradx =
5937:         (at::special_i0e(safe_self) -
5938:          result * (safe_self.sgn() + safe_self.reciprocal()));
5939:     return grad *
5940:         at::where(
5941:                self_is_not_tiny, gradx, at::scalar_tensor(0.5, self.options()));
5942:   });
5943: }
```

```cpp
5953: // Forward AD:
5954: //
5955: // Let X := U^{-1} L^{-1} P^T B be the output of the function.
5956: // Also, the LU input of the function could be represented as
5957: // LU = (L - I) + U.
5958: //
5959: // Differentiating LU = L + U - I produces:
5960: // dLU = dL + dU.
5961: // Noting that dL and dU are lower- and upper-triangular, respectively,
5962: // and that the diagonal of L is never explicitly exposed, so
5963: // diag(dL) = 0, it follows
5964: // dL = dLU * 1_L,
5965: // dU = dLU * 1_U.
5966: //
5967: // Differentiating X = U^{-1} L^{-1} P^T B produces:
5968: // dX = dU^{-1} L^{-1} P^T B + U^{-1} dL^{-1} P^T B + U^{-1} L^{-1} P^T dB
5969: // Note that for any invertible matrix A we have A A^{-1} = I, hence
5970: // dA A^{-1} + A dA^{-1} = 0 => dA^{-1} = -A^{-1} dA A^{-1}.
5971: // Inserting it back into the definition of dX gives:
5972: // dX = -U^{-1} dU U^{-1} L^{-1} P^T B - U^{-1} L^{-1} dL L^{-1} P^T B + U^{-1}
5973: // L^{-1} P^T dB dX = -U^{-1} dU X - U^{-1} L^{-1} dL U X + U^{-1} L^{-1} P^T dB
5974: //
5975: // Backward AD:
5976: //
5977: // Using the definition of dL, dU from above:
```

```cpp
6098:         U, dLU.triu() + R.matmul(U), /*upper*/ true);
6099:     // dX = op_2(R op_2(X)) + S
6100:     return (left ? R.matmul(X) : X.matmul(R.mH())) + S;
6101:   } else {
6102:     // We see that when left == adjoint, op_1(A) = A^H
6103:     // dX = op_2(op_1(-op_3(B)^H U^{-1}(dUU^{-1} + L^{-1}dL)L^{-1} P^T)) + S
6104:     // Now, note that whenever adjoint == left, we have that
6105:     // op_3(B)^H A^{-1} = op_3(X)^H
6106:     // We can then rewrite the formula above in terms of X as
6107:     // dX = op_2(op_1(-op_3(X)^H P(LdUU^{-1} + dL)L^{-1} P^T)) + S
6108:     auto [P, L, U] = at::lu_unpack(LU, pivots);
6109:     // Compute V = op_3(X)^H
6110:     auto V = left ? X.mH() : X;
6111:     // Compute the inner parens LdUU^{-1} + dL
6112:     auto R = at::linalg_solve_triangular(
6113:                  U, L.matmul(dLU.triu()), /*upper*/ true, /*left*/ false) +
6114:         dLU.tril(-1);
6115:     // dX = op_2(op_1(-op_3(X)^H PRL^{-1} P^T)) + S
6116:     R = at::linalg_solve_triangular(
6117:             L,
6118:             -V.matmul(P).matmul(R),
6119:             /*upper*/ false,
6120:             /*left*/ false,
6121:             /*unitriangular*/ true)
6122:             .matmul(P.mT());
6123:     // dX = op_2(R^H) + S
6124:     return (left ? R.mH() : std::move(R)) + S;
6125:   }
6126: }
```

```cpp
6355: Tensor cumprod_jvp(
6356:     const Tensor& self_t,
6357:     const Tensor& self_p,
6358:     const Tensor& result,
6359:     int dim) {
6360:   // Generic formula when no 0. is involved
6361:   Tensor gradient = (self_t / self_p).cumsum(dim) * result;
6362: 
6363:   // Note that we have to use at::where below as we are removing nans
6364: 
6365:   if (self_p.dim() == 0) {
6366:     gradient.masked_fill_(self_p.eq(0), self_t);
6367:     return gradient;
6368:   } else {
6369:     // For input (a, 0, b, 0, c) with gradients (t0, t1, t2, t3, t4)
6370:     // The output of cumprod is (a, 0, 0, 0, 0)
6371:     // The gradient we want to compute is (t0, a*t1, a*b*t1, 0, 0)
6372:     // We do this by:
6373:     // Get a mask of all zeros (0, 1, 0, 1, 0)
6374:     auto mask_zeros = self_p.eq(0);
6375:     // Get a mask of the first zero for each dim (0, 1, 0, 0, 0)
6376:     auto mask_first_zero = mask_zeros.logical_and(mask_zeros.cumsum(dim).eq(1));
6377: 
6378:     // Get the new grad value that should be used after any zero happened:
6379:     // (X, a*t1, a*b*t1, 0, 0) = cumprod((a, t1, b, 0, c))
6380:     auto new_grad = at::where(mask_first_zero, self_t, self_p).cumprod(dim);
6381: 
6382:     // Get a mask of everything after the first zero: (0, 1, 1, 1, 1)
6383:     auto mask_after_first_zero = mask_first_zero.cumsum(dim);
6384: 
6385:     // Do the final replacement
6386:     return at::where(
6387:         mask_after_first_zero.to(ScalarType::Bool), new_grad, gradient);
6388:   }
6389: }
```

```cpp
6771: // Useful notation:
```

```cpp
6785: // Forward AD:
6786: //
6787: // dA = P dL U + P L dU => [left-multiply P^T]
6788: // (P^T dA1 | P^T dA2) = (dL U1 + L dU1 | dL U2 + L dU2) (*)
6789: // From (*):
6790: // P^T dA1 = dL U1 + L dU1 => [left-multiply by L^{-1}, right-multiply by
6791: // U1^{-1}] L^{-1} P^T dA1 U1^{-1} = L^{-1} dL + dU1 U1^{-1} (**). Note, L is
6792: // lower-triangular, and so is its inverse, hence L^{-1} dL is lower-triangular.
6793: // Also, since the diagonal of L (all ones) is never exposed explicitly (packed
6794: // representation), the diagonal of dL is zero, and hence diag(L^{-1} dL) = 0.
6795: // Assuming that U1 is full-rank, similarly, dU1 U1^{-1} is upper-triangular.
6796: // Combining these observations we conclude:
6797: //
6798: // L^{-1} dL = (L^{-1} P^T dA1 U1^{-1}) o 1_L,
6799: // dU1 U1^{-1} = (L^{-1} P^T dA1 U1^{-1}) o 1_U.
6800: //
6801: // Hence,
6802: // dL = L [(L^{-1} P^T dA1 U1^{-1}) o 1_L],
6803: // dU1 = [(L^{-1} P^T dA1 U1^{-1}) o 1_U] U1.
6804: // As for dU2, from (*) it follows
6805: // P^T dA2 = dL U2 + L dU2 =>
6806: // dU2 = L^{-1} (P^T dA2 - dL U2).
6807: //
6808: // Backward AD:
6809: //
6810: // The following equality comes very handy:
6811: // Tr(A (B o C)) = Tr((A o B^T) C) (!)
6812: // or in other words, given that X -> B o X is a pointwise operation
6813: // its Jacobian is diagonal, so its differential is self-adjoint
6814: // <A, B o C> = <A o B, C>
6815: //
6816: // Tr(A_grad^H dA) = Tr(L_grad^H dL) + Tr(U_grad^H dU), then
6817: //
6818: // Tr(L_grad^H dL) = Tr(L_grad^H L [(L^{-1} P^T dA1 U1^{-1}) o 1_L] = [using
6819: // (!)]
6820: //                 = Tr((L_grad^H L o 1_L^T) L^{-1} P^T dA1 U1^{-1}) = [using
6821: //                 the cyclic property of Tr] = Tr(U1^{-1} (L_grad^H L o 1_L^T)
6822: //                 L^{-1} P^T dA1)
6823: //
6824: // Similar, using (!) and the cyclic property of the trace operator:
6825: // Tr(U_grad^H dU) = Tr(U1_grad^H dU1) + Tr(U2_grad^H dU2)
6826: //                 = Tr(U1^{-1} (U1 U1_grad^H o 1_U^T) L^{-1} P^T dA1)
6827: //                   + Tr(U2_grad^H L^{-1} P^T dA2)
6828: //                   - Tr(U1^{-1} (U2 U2_grad^H o 1_L^T) L^{-1} P^T dA1)
6829: //
6830: // By combining the matrices to the left from dA1 and dA2 and then applying
6831: // conjugate transposition, we finally arrive at:
6832: //
6833: // A1_grad = P L^{-H} [L^H L_grad o 1_L + U1_grad U1^H o 1_U - U2_grad U2^H o
```

```cpp
6994: 
6995:   // similar to the backward implementation, we also consider block structures
6996:   // such as: for a matrix A of size m x n we decompose it as A = (A1 | A2) with
6997:   // A1 of size m x m if m <= n and A = (A1^T | A2^T)^T with A1 of size n x n if
6998:   // m > n.

7000:   auto L1 = L.narrow(-2, 0, k).narrow(-1, 0, k);
7001:   auto U1 = U.narrow(-2, 0, k).narrow(-1, 0, k);
7002: 
7003:   // We form using two triangular_solve the matrix, the second one in place
7004:   // dK = L1^{-1} PdA1 U2^{-1}

7006:       L1, PdA1, /*upper=*/false, /*left=*/true, /*unitriangular*/ true);
```

```cpp
7046: Tensor lu_factor_ex_jvp(
7047:     const Tensor& dA,
7048:     const Tensor& LU,
7049:     const Tensor& pivs,
7050:     const bool pivot) {
7051:   auto [P, L, U] =
7052:       at::lu_unpack(LU, pivs, /*unpack_data=*/true, /*unpack_pivots=*/pivot);
7053:   auto [dL, dU] = linalg_lu_jvp(dA, P, L, U, pivot);
7054: 
7055:   auto m = dA.size(-2);
7056:   auto n = dA.size(-1);
7057:   if (m >= n) {
7058:     dL.narrow(-2, 0, n).add_(dU);
7059:     return dL;
7060:   } else {
7061:     dU.narrow(-1, 0, m).add_(dL);
7062:     return dU;
7063:   }
7064: }

7066: Tensor logsumexp_jvp(
7067:     const Tensor& self_p,
7068:     const Tensor& self_t,
7069:     IntArrayRef dim,
7070:     bool keepdim) {
7071:   // NB: for simplicity, we recompute some values that can be reused from
7072:   // forward
7073:   auto self_p_exp = [&self_p, &dim]() {
7074:     if (self_p.sym_numel() > 0) {
7075:       // Use only the real part for complex tensors
7076:       return (self_p - at::amax(at::real(self_p), dim, true))
7077:           .exp(); // Use the exp-normalize trick
7078:     } else {
7079:       // amax fails if numel() == 0, in which case it doesn't matter anyway
7080:       return self_p.exp();
7081:     }
7082:   }();
7083: 
7084:   auto sumexp_p = self_p_exp.sum(dim, keepdim);
7085: 
7086:   // NB: it's OK for logsumexp_jvp to be reused for formulas like
7087:   // softmax/log_softmax
7088:   //     that only have one differentiable input, because that means self_t are
7089:   //     never zerotensors
7090:   TORCH_INTERNAL_ASSERT(!self_t._is_zerotensor())
7091:   if (areAnyTensorSubclassLike({self_p, self_t})) {
7092:     auto result = (self_p_exp * self_t).sum(dim, keepdim);
7093:     result /= sumexp_p;
7094:     return result;
7095:   } else {
7096:     self_p_exp *= self_t;
7097:     auto sumexp_t = self_p_exp.sum(dim, keepdim);
7098:     return sumexp_t /= sumexp_p;
7099:   }
7100: }
7101:
```

```cpp
7377: Tensor take_backward(
7378:     const Tensor& grad,
7379:     const Tensor& self,
7380:     const Tensor& indices) {
```

```cpp
7573: namespace {
7574: 
7575: // Bound integer tap coordinate per padding mode (for bicubic taps).
7576: // Returns a kLong tensor in [0, size-1].
7577: static Tensor gs_bound_coord(
7578:     const Tensor& idx,
7579:     int64_t size,
7580:     GridSamplerPadding padding_mode,
7581:     bool align_corners) {
7582:   if (padding_mode != GridSamplerPadding::Reflection) {
7583:     return idx.clamp(0, size - 1);
7584:   }
7585:   // Reflection: mirrors the PyTorch reflect_coordinates convention.
7586:   if (size <= 1) {
7587:     return at::zeros_like(idx);
7588:   }
7589:   auto x = idx.to(at::kDouble);
7590:   double span, min_v;
7591:   if (align_corners) {
7592:     span = size - 1;
7593:     min_v = 0.0;
7594:   } else {
7595:     span = size;
7596:     min_v = -0.5;
7597:   }
7598:   // Native formula: in = idx - min_v (= idx + 0.5 for no-align_corners).
7599:   // For integer idx this is always a half-integer, so fold result + min_v is
7600:   // an exact integer — no rounding artifacts.
7601:   auto in = x - min_v;
7602:   auto in_abs = in.abs();
7603:   auto even = at::fmod(at::floor(in_abs / span), 2.0) < 0.5;
7604:   auto rem = at::fmod(in_abs, span);
7605:   auto r = at::where(even, rem, span - rem);
7606:   return (r + min_v).round().clamp(0, size - 1).to(at::kLong);
7607: }
```

```cpp
7712: // Multi-tap bounded gather for bicubic 2D: h_idx/w_idx [N, Ho, Wo, K] → [N, C,

7714: static Tensor gs_gather2d_bc_multi(
7715:     const Tensor& input,
7716:     const Tensor& h_idx,
7717:     const Tensor& w_idx,
7718:     GridSamplerPadding padding_mode,
7719:     bool align_corners) {
7720:   auto N = input.size(0), C = input.size(1);
7721:   auto H = input.size(2), W = input.size(3);
7722:   auto out_H = h_idx.size(1), out_W = h_idx.size(2), K = h_idx.size(3);
7723:   auto flat = (gs_bound_coord(h_idx, H, padding_mode, align_corners) * W +
7724:                gs_bound_coord(w_idx, W, padding_mode, align_corners))
7725:                   .reshape({N, 1, out_H * out_W * K})
7726:                   .expand({N, C, out_H * out_W * K});
7727:   auto result = input.reshape({N, C, H * W})
7728:                     .gather(2, flat)
7729:                     .reshape({N, C, out_H, out_W, K});
7730:   if (padding_mode == GridSamplerPadding::Zeros) {
7731:     auto mask = (h_idx >= 0) & (h_idx < H) & (w_idx >= 0) & (w_idx < W);
7732:     result = result * mask.unsqueeze(1).to(result.dtype());
7733:   }
7734:   return result;
7735: }
```
